 M 3 -Bench: Multi-Modal, Multi-Hop, Multi-Threaded Tool-Using MLLM Agent Benchmark 
 
 
 
Yang Zhou Mingyu Zhao 1 1 footnotemark: 1  Zhenting Wang Difei Gu Bangwei Guo 
 Ruosong Ye Ligong Han Can Jin Dimitris N. Metaxas
 
Rutgers University
 {eta.yang, zhenting.wang, bangwei.guo}@rutgers.edu
 {ligong.han, can.jin, dnm}@rutgers.edu
 {mz751, dg1188, ry233}@scarletmail.rutgers.edu 
 Equal contribution. 
 
 
 Abstract 
 We present M 3 -Bench, the first benchmark for evaluating multimodal tool use under the Model Context Protocol.
The benchmark targets realistic, multi-hop and multi-threaded workflows that require visual grounding and textual reasoning, cross-tool dependencies, and persistence of intermediate resources across steps.
We introduce a similarity-driven alignment that serializes each tool call, embeds signatures with a sentence encoder, and performs similarity-bucketed Hungarian matching to obtain auditable one-to-one correspondences.
On top of this alignment, we report interpretable metrics that decouple semantic fidelity from workflow consistency. The benchmark spans 27 servers with 232 tools, and provides standardized trajectories curated through an Executor & Judge pipeline with human verification;
an auxiliary four large language models (LLMs) judge ensemble reports end-task Task Completion and information grounding. Evaluations of representative state-of-the-art Multimodal LLMs (MLLMs) reveal persistent gaps in multimodal MCP tool use, particularly in argument fidelity and structure consistency, underscoring the need for methods that jointly reason over images, text, and tool graphs. Our Benchmark’s anonymous repository is at https://github.com/EtaYang10th/Open-M3-Bench 
 
 
 
 1 Introduction 
 
 Figure 1 : Summary leaderboard of state-of-the-art (SOTA) MLLMs on M 3 -Bench (higher is better). 
 
 
 We introduce M 3 -Bench, the Multi-Modal , Multiplex , Matching-aware MCP Benchmark, as a principled evaluation suite for multimodal tool use under the Model Context Protocol (MCP). Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have recently shown that, once they are allowed to perform function calling (tool use), they can query external services and reason over information that is not contained in their parameters [ 42 , 50 , 43 , 33 , 8 , 5 , 48 , 20 , 53 , 7 ] . MCP specifies how models communicate with heterogeneous tools through standardized servers, which makes execution more reproducible across systems [ 29 ] . However, existing MCP benchmarks are mostly text-only and focus on linear API planning or database queries [ 36 , 34 , 49 , 13 , 24 , 25 , 12 , 40 ] . A systematic evaluation of multimodal MCP workflows, where images and text jointly condition tool calls and results, is still missing [ 2 , 25 ] . 
 
 
 
 
 
 (a) 
 
 
 
 
 (b) 
 
 
 
 Figure 2 : Overview of tasks and a realistic multimodal MCP workflow. (a) Representative task samples in M 3 -Bench. (b) A realistic multimodal MCP workflow: the agent (i) calls a vision MCP server to detect and mark hazards in the input image, (ii) calls Imagesorcery MCP to process the image, and (iii) calls an office/productivity MCP server to create and populate a slide deck. 
 
 
 The core challenge in real-world MCP trajectories is visual grounding: multimodal tool invocation hinges on correctly interpreting the image before any tool can be parameterized. In 2(a) , the agent receives a photograph and must first resolve the landmark/city from visual cues, only then can it condition subsequent MCP calls; The agent must fully recognize the fully stocked and well-displayed products on the shelf in a photograph before proceeding to the next MCP tools call.
Second, real-world MCP trajectories are not single-shot calls. They are multi-hop, they contain causally dependent operations, and they frequently execute several tool calls in parallel within one step [ 27 , 54 , 22 , 52 ] . The task in 2(b) illustrates this setting. The agent receives an image of a hazardous construction scene and a reporting-style instruction.
Inside one step, actions that do not depend on each other, such as adding an image and updating bullets, can be executed concurrently. Across steps, operations that do depend on earlier results, such as annotating before inserting or creating the deck before saving, must follow the ground-truth order. These properties make simple string matching or linear-sequence scoring inadequate [ 27 , 54 ] , and it is precisely what our benchmark aims to stress. We define two important concepts in what follows: Multi-Hop refers to workflows with more than one causally dependent step, where later actions consume artifacts produced earlier [ 51 , 14 ] ; Multi-Threaded refers to order-independent tool calls executed within a single step under shared state, allowing safe parallelism while preserving cross-step causality (Mathematical definition in 1 & 2 ).

 Table 1 : Comparisons to existing tool-using benchmarks (compact single-column). 
 
 
 Table 1 contrasts M 3 -Bench with prior tool-use evaluations along 8 axes.
 Domains and Tools enumerate functional breadth and unique tool count.
 MCP ecosystem marks whether the benchmark connect the agent directly to a set of production-grade, live MCP servers, and Information grounding indicates that answers must be supported by evidence returned from tools.
 Fuzzy task description denotes underspecified, natural instructions without a clear trajectory. Critically, Multi-Hop & Threaded captures cross-step causal dependencies together with safe within-step parallelism; Multimodality requires joint image–text conditioning; and Similarity Metric denotes large language models (LLM) free, similarity-aware alignment of predicted to reference calls (see section 3 ). As discussed above, Multimodality and Multi-Hop/Threaded causality are central to realistic MCP workflows, and a Similarity-Based scorer is necessary to credit semantically correct calls. Most existing benchmarks are text-only and/or lack explicit multi-threaded causality, and none pair these with a similarity-aware alignment; in contrast, M 3 -Bench satisfies all three while retaining the key advantages of prior benchmarks. 
 
 
 We align predicted and reference tool calls with a similarity-bucketed Hungarian alignment : each call is serialized, embedded with a fixed sentence encoder, and one-to-one matched within tool-name buckets under weak/strong cosine thresholds. This gives deterministic, auditable correspondences without relying on an LLM judge for call-level scoring.
On top of this alignment, we report a compact, recall-aware suite that separates section 3 . A small four-model judge ensemble is used for evaluating the overall quality of the trajectory to retain some of the advantages of the LLM judge feature.
Besides, to standardize references, our experiments also provide a best trajectory obtained via an Executor–Judge loop. 
 
 
 Contributions. 
 
 
 
 1. 
 
 We present, to our knowledge, the first benchmark explicitly targeting multimodal MCP workflows. 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 Our repository provides an end-to-end pipeline for generating MCP best trajectories, with an optional lightweight human verification pass to enhance stability. 
 
 
 
 3. 
 
 We introduce a structure-aware metric suite that aligns tool calls via bucketed Hungarian matching on high-dimensional feature similarities, yielding robust and auditable correspondences for effectively diagnosing distinct error modes. 
 
 
 
 4. 
 
 We evaluate a wide range of mainstream MLLMs and conduct a comprehensive assessment across tasks, budgets, error types, and other factors. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 2 Related Work 
 
 
 2.1 LLM Tool Use and Protocols 
 
 Early studies showed that large language models (LLMs) can autonomously decide when/what/how to call external tools via simple APIs [ 42 , 27 , 1 , 36 , 28 , 17 , 18 , 19 , 10 , 9 ] . Reliability improves with API-aware finetuning and retrieval [ 35 ] ; instruction/data scaling expands tool repertoires and evaluations (ToolBench) [ 36 ] ; and hierarchical retrieval with self-reflection addresses large tool spaces and brittle protocols (AnyTool/AnyToolBench) [ 11 ] . Benchmarks targeting the decision to use a tool and tool selection (MetaTool Benchmark) complement these directions [ 16 ] . To reduce integration friction across heterogeneous tools, the Model Context Protocol (MCP) standardizes interfaces [ 2 , 29 ] . 
 
 
 
 
 2.2 Benchmarks for Tool Use and MCP 
 
 Beyond general agent evaluations such as AgentBench [ 23 ] and web-agent suites like WebArena and BrowserGym [ 54 , 21 ] , recent MCP-native efforts emphasize realistic, multi-hop use over live servers: MCP-Bench links LLMs to dozens of MCP servers and hundreds of tools [ 45 ] ; MCP-Universe provides an end-to-end framework grounded in real-world servers and long-horizon tasks [ 39 , 38 ] ; MCPToolBench++ scales tool categories and tasks via marketplace-sourced servers [ 12 ] ; and MCP-Atlas curates a human-authored dataset and leaderboard for multi-tool workflows [ 40 , 41 ] . Relative to these, we target multimodal tasks and introduce a similarity-aware alignment with structure-sensitive diagnostics. 
 
 
 Figure 3 : Benchmark trajectory generation and judging pipeline.
The Executor plans tools, executes calls with controlled multi-threaded execution, and summarizes; the Judge reconstructs an optimal trajectory and verifies correctness. where c ℓ , n ℓ c_{\ell,n_{\ell}} denotes the n ℓ n_{\ell} -th tool call in step ℓ \ell . 
 
 
 
 
 
 3 M 3 -Bench 
 
 
 3.1 Benchmark Construction 
 
 We generate benchmark trajectories with an Executor–Judge loop that decouples tool execution from adjudication ( Figure 3 ). For each image–task pair, a human instruction fixes intent and permissible tools. Given the prompt, image, and registry, the Executor produces a stepwise trajectory; the Judge audits and consolidates it into a standardized optimal trajectory for alignment. All prompts are in Appendix subsection 6.2 . 
 
 
 Via system prompts, the Executor receives the full tool list (with docstrings) and outputs the planned tool set for the current step— selection only to stabilize reasoning and keep the chain controllable. (See Appendix Prompt: Plan .) 
 
 
 Process 
 
 The Executor returns strictly valid JSON tool_calls (each with name and arguments ). A multi-hop workflow allows multi-threaded interaction with the MCP Ecosystem within a single step. Structured outputs are appended to the dialogue, and any file-image paths from MCP tools persist in a temporary workspace for later steps. (See Appendix Prompt: Process .) 
 
 
 
 Decision and Final Reply 
 
 After each step, a binary prompt checks whether evidence suffices to conclude; if so, the Executor emits only the final natural-language answer. (See Appendix Prompt: Decision , Prompt: Final .) 
 
 
 
 Judge and Summarize 
 
 The Judge consumes the initial prompt, image, and full Executor dialogue (including the tool-call chain). Trajectories with causal misordering or factual errors are rejected; otherwise, the Judge reconstructs the optimal trajectory , removing failed/redundant calls and merging operations that could run concurrently. Human verifiers audit scope and logical consistency; the audited result becomes the benchmark reference. (See Appendix Prompt: Judge , Prompt: Summarize .) 
 
 
 Figure 4 : Overview of MCP server ecosystem used in the M 3 -Bench. Category distribution of MCP servers; and tool distribution across servers. The inventory follows the MCP design and public specification. 
 
 
 
 
 
 3.2 Task Distribution 
 
 We construct a curated suite of 28 multimodal MCP tasks; detailed per-task goals and input–output constraints are provided in Appendix subsection 6.1 , and representative full trajectories appear in section 7 . In total, the evaluation set contains 211 trajectories, whose reference trajectories comprise 644 steps and 1337 MCP tool calls. 
 
 
 
 
 3.3 MCP Ecosystem 
 
 To keep trajectories controllable and reproducible, we curate a compact set of MCP servers and tools with minimal functional overlap.
We favor orthogonal, well-documented, and stable utilities, discarding redundant or unstable options to reduce confounds during evaluation.
Our benchmark spans 232 representative tools across 27 MCP servers ( Figure 4 (a)).
This diverse ecosystem covers Academic & Knowledge, Science & Space, Geography & Travel, E-commerce & Finance, Office Automation, Weather & Air Quality, and Health, enabling broad capability coverage in M 3 -Bench (see Figure 4 (b)). 
 
 
 
 
 
 4 Metrics 
 
 In subsection 4.1 , we first establish the mathematical definitions and foundations for our metrics. Building on these theoretical bases, we then construct the Detection Metrics subsection 4.2 , Trajectory Alignment Metrics subsection 4.3 , and LLM Judges Metrics subsection 4.4 . 
 
 
 
 4.1 Mathematical foundations 
 
 A trajectory is a DAG ordered by turns ( steps ). Denote L L as the number of steps in a trajectory 𝒯 \mathcal{T} . The ground-truth 𝒯 ⋆ \mathcal{T}^{\star} and predicted trajectories 𝒯 ^ \widehat{\mathcal{T}} are:
 𝒯 ⋆ = { S 1 ⋆ , … , S L ⋆ ⋆ } \mathcal{T}^{\star}=\{S^{\star}_{1},\ldots,S^{\star}_{L^{\star}}\} ,
 𝒯 ^ = { S ^ 1 , … , S ^ L ^ } \widehat{\mathcal{T}}=\{\widehat{S}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{S}_{\widehat{L}}\} ,
where each step S S enumerates its calls c c ,
 S ℓ ⋆ = { c ℓ , 1 ⋆ , … , c ℓ , n ℓ ⋆ ⋆ } S^{\star}_{\ell}=\{\,c^{\star}_{\ell,1},\ldots,c^{\star}_{\ell,n^{\star}_{\ell}}\,\} ,
 S ^ ℓ ^ = { c ^ ℓ ^ , 1 , … , c ^ ℓ ^ , n ℓ ^ } . \widehat{S}_{\hat{\ell}}=\{\,\hat{c}_{\hat{\ell},1},\ldots,\hat{c}_{\hat{\ell},n_{\hat{\ell}}}\,\}. 
where ℓ \ell is the step of the trajectory, so n ℓ ⋆ n^{\star}_{\ell} and n ℓ ^ n_{\hat{\ell}} are per-step call counts. A call is c = ( tool ​ ( c ) , args ​ ( c ) ) , c=(\mathrm{\texttt{tool}}(c),\mathrm{\texttt{args}}(c)), 
where tool ​ ( ⋅ ) \mathrm{\texttt{tool}}(\cdot) is the categorical tool identifier and args ​ ( ⋅ ) \mathrm{\texttt{args}}(\cdot) serializes all deterministic inputs. 
 
 
 
 Definition 1 (Multi-Hop) . 
 
 
 Let 𝒯 = { S 1 , … , S L } \mathcal{T}=\{S_{1},\dots,S_{L}\} be a tool-use trajectory with steps S ℓ = { c ℓ , 1 , … , c ℓ , n ℓ } S_{\ell}=\{c_{\ell,1},\dots,c_{\ell,n_{\ell}}\} and inter-call dependency edges across steps.
We call 𝒯 \mathcal{T} multi-hop if L ≥ 2 L\geq 2 and there exists a dependency ( c i , a → c j , b ) (c_{i,a}\!\to\!c_{j,b}) with i < j i<j , where a , b ∈ ℕ a,b\in\mathbb{N} and a ∈ [ 1 , n i ] , b ∈ [ 1 , n j ] a\in[1,n_{i}],\ b\in[1,n_{j}] . 
 
 
 
 
 Definition 2 (Multi-Threaded) . 
 
 
 A step S ℓ S_{\ell} is multi-threaded if it contains at least two calls c ℓ , a ≠ c ℓ , b c_{\ell,a}\neq c_{\ell,b} that are order-independent given the shared state at depth ℓ \ell (no dependency edge between them). 
 
 
 
 These definitions make explicit that a trajectory is a structured object with cross-step causal edges and within-step parallelism. A general option is to delegate this alignment to an LLM judge, but it introduces (i) circularity/lineage bias , where judges trained like the systems under test tend to over-credit them; (ii) susceptibility to prompt gaming , as long rationales or CoT responses can inflate scores even when the underlying tool use is wrong; (iii) weak argument verification , since judges rarely check whether argument objects are semantically correct or whether order-invariant sets are handled; (iv) knowledge substitution , where missing tool evidence is replaced by the model’s own world knowledge; and (v) opacity , as scores lack auditable, one-to-one correspondences between predicted and reference calls. Real MCP trajectories exacerbate these issues: steps often contain unordered sets of calls, later calls can self-correct earlier errors, and partial success is common. Consequently, the alignment primitive must be deterministic, auditable, and robust to surface-form variation. 
 
 
 Similarity-Bucketed Hungarian Alignment 
 
 To address this problem, we introduce a similarity computation method based on a text encoder.
Each call is flattened to a canonical text string by a serialization function ϕ ​ ( ⋅ ) \phi(\cdot) that preserves tool name, required arguments and order-invariant sets.
We embed serialized calls with a sentence encoder ℰ \mathcal{E} and compute cosine similarity:
 S i ​ j = ⟨ 𝐱 i ⋆ , 𝐱 ^ j ⟩ ∥ 𝐱 i ⋆ ∥ 2 ​ ∥ 𝐱 ^ j ∥ 2 ∈ [ − 1 , 1 ] S_{ij}=\frac{\langle\mathbf{x}^{\star}_{i},\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{j}\rangle}{\lVert\mathbf{x}^{\star}_{i}\rVert_{2}\,\lVert\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{j}\rVert_{2}}\in[-1,1] ,
where
 𝐱 i ⋆ = ℰ ​ ( ϕ ​ ( c i ⋆ ) ) \mathbf{x}^{\star}_{i}=\mathcal{E}\!\big(\phi(c^{\star}_{i})\big) and
 𝐱 ^ j = ℰ ​ ( ϕ ​ ( c ^ j ) ) \widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{j}=\mathcal{E}\!\big(\phi(\hat{c}_{j})\big) . 
 
 
 We align with a tool-specific, step-agnostic assignment that yields auditable one-to-one correspondences. Concretely, we serialize every call into a canonical text signature and embed it with the sentence encoder SentenceTransformers [ 37 ] from Textual Encoding and Similarity , giving a similarity matrix S S . For each tool k k , we form the bucketed index sets
 ℐ k = { i ∣ tool ​ ( c i ⋆ ) = k } \mathcal{I}_{k}=\{\,i\mid\mathrm{\texttt{tool}}(c^{\star}_{i})=k\,\} ,
 𝒥 ^ k = { j ∣ tool ​ ( c ^ j ) = k } \widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{k}=\{\,j\mid\mathrm{\texttt{tool}}(\hat{c}_{j})=k\,\} ,
so that only same-tool pairs are considered. We convert similarity to cost with a weak screening threshold τ weak \tau_{\mathrm{weak}} to forbid low-similarity edges: 
 
 
 
 C r ​ c ( k ) = { 1 − S r ​ c ( k ) , S r ​ c ( k ) ≥ τ weak , λ pen , S r ​ c ( k ) < τ weak , C^{(k)}_{rc}=\begin{cases}1-S^{(k)}_{rc},&S^{(k)}_{rc}\geq\tau_{\mathrm{weak}},\\
\lambda_{\mathrm{pen}},&S^{(k)}_{rc}<\tau_{\mathrm{weak}},\end{cases} 
 
 (1) 
 
 
 and solve a linear assignment in each bucket via the Hungarian (Kuhn–Munkres) algorithm min Π ( k ) ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ⟨ C ( k ) , Π ( k ) ⟩ \min_{\Pi^{(k)}\in\mathcal{P}}\ \langle C^{(k)},\Pi^{(k)}\rangle 
retaining only feasible pairs above the weak threshold to form the global match set:
 M = { ( i , j , S i ​ j ) ∣ S i ​ j ≥ τ weak } . M=\{(i,j,S_{ij})\ \mid\ S_{ij}\geq\tau_{\mathrm{weak}}\}. 
 
 
 
 Properties and rationale. 
 
 Tool bucketing prevents cross-tool credit; step agnosticism allows early-wrong, later-correct fixes to match without forcing same-step alignment; and similarity gating recognizes paraphrase and order-invariant arguments without inflating coverage. Together, these choices deliver: (i) deterministic, auditable correspondences in place of LLM judge; (ii) invariance to within-step call permutations (sets are unordered); and (iii) faithful scoring under partial success , misaligned or missing calls simply remain unmatched, while correctly recovered calls still receive credit. This alignment thus targets exactly the failure modes we observe in MCP trajectories while satisfying the three requirements in subsection 4.1 . 
 
 
 Figure 5 : Overview of our structure-sensitive metrics. where S ℓ ⋆ S^{\star}_{\ell} and S ^ ℓ ^ \widehat{S}_{\hat{\ell}} denote the sets of calls in the ground-truth step ℓ \ell and the predicted step ℓ ^ \hat{\ell} , respectively, and c ℓ , n ℓ ⋆ ⋆ c^{\star}_{\ell,n^{\star}_{\ell}} and c ^ ℓ ^ , n ℓ ^ \hat{c}_{\hat{\ell},n_{\hat{\ell}}} denote the n ℓ ⋆ n^{\star}_{\ell} -th ground-truth call in step ℓ \ell and the n ℓ ^ n_{\hat{\ell}} -th predicted call in step ℓ ^ \hat{\ell} . 
 
 
 
 
 
 4.2 Detection Metrics 
 
 Based on the above mathematical foundations, we introduce detection-based metrics: 
 
 
 1. 
 
 Systems may call incorrect tools or omit required ones, motivating Recall ; 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 MLLM may make unnecessary calls, resulting in additional waste of resources Precision . 
 
 
 
 
 
 Recall and Precision. 
 
 ( Figure 5 (a)-(b))
These metrics quantify, respectively, how many ground-truth calls are successfully matched (coverage) and how many predicted calls are valid (false-positive control). Given per-sample match sets M m {M_{m}} with counts N gt , m N_{\mathrm{gt},m} and N pred , m N_{\mathrm{pred},m} , we define
 Recall = ∑ m | M m | ∑ m N gt , m \mathrm{Recall}=\frac{\sum_{m}|M_{m}|}{\sum_{m}N_{\mathrm{gt},m}} ,
 Precision = ∑ m | M m | ∑ m N pred , m \mathrm{Precision}=\frac{\sum_{m}|M_{m}|}{\sum_{m}N_{\mathrm{pred},m}} ,
where | M m | |M_{m}| counts matched pairs for sample m m , N gt = ∑ ℓ n ℓ ⋆ N_{\mathrm{gt}}=\sum_{\ell}n^{\star}_{\ell} and N pred = ∑ ℓ ^ n ℓ ^ N_{\mathrm{pred}}=\sum_{\hat{\ell}}n_{\hat{\ell}} be the total numbers of calls in 𝒯 ⋆ \mathcal{T}^{\star} and 𝒯 ^ \widehat{\mathcal{T}} , respectively. 
 
 
 
 
 
 4.3 Trajectory Alignment Metrics 
 
 Next, we introduce a metric to quantify the degree of Trajectory Alignment. 
 
 
 1. 
 
 MLLM may select the correct tool but supply semantically incorrect arguments, requiring an Average Similarity measure over serialized calls; 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 MLLM may unnecessarily split operations that could run in a single step in a multi-threaded scenario, captured by Step Coherence ; 
 
 
 
 3. 
 
 MLLM may collapse causally distinct multi-hop subgoals into one step in a multi-hop scenario, quantified by Merge Purity ; 
 
 
 
 4. 
 
 MLLM may invert the execution order of causally dependent steps in a multi-hop scenario, detected by Order Consistency . 
 
 
 
 
 
 Argument Similarity. 
 
 We summarize the semantic faithfulness of matched arguments as 
 
 
 
 ArgSim = ∑ ( i , j ) ∈ M , S i ​ j ≥ τ strong S i ​ j | { ( i , j ) ∈ M : S i ​ j ≥ τ strong } | , \mathrm{ArgSim}=\frac{\sum_{(i,j)\in M,\,S_{ij}\geq\tau_{\mathrm{strong}}}S_{ij}}{|\{(i,j)\in M:S_{ij}\geq\tau_{\mathrm{strong}}\}|}, 
 
 (2) 
 
 
 where τ strong \tau_{\mathrm{strong}} controls alignment confidence.

 Step Coherence. 
 
 ( Figure 5 (c))
This metric penalizes scattering matches from one ground-truth step across multiple predicted steps, reflecting unnecessary splits of operations that could have been executed within a single multi-threaded step. For any call c c , let gt st ​ ( c ) \mathrm{gt_{st}}(c) and pred st ​ ( c ) \mathrm{pred_{st}}(c) denote its step indices in the ground-truth and predicted trajectories. For each ground-truth step ℓ \ell , collect the predicted step indices touched by its matches:
 𝒫 ​ ( ℓ ) = { pred st ​ ( c ^ j ) ∣ ( i , j ) ∈ M , gt st ​ ( c i ⋆ ) = ℓ } \mathcal{P}(\ell)=\{\ \mathrm{pred_{st}}(\hat{c}_{j})\ \mid\ (i,j)\in M,\mathrm{gt_{st}}(c^{\star}_{i})=\ell\ \} .Then we define the per-step coherence and a similarity-weighted global average as 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 sc ​ ( ℓ ) = { 1 , | 𝒫 ​ ( ℓ ) | ≤ 1 , 1 | 𝒫 ​ ( ℓ ) | , otherwise , \displaystyle\mathrm{sc}(\ell)= 
 
 (3) 
 
 
 
 
 StepCoh = ∑ ℓ w ℓ ​ sc ​ ( ℓ ) ∑ ℓ w ℓ , \displaystyle\mathrm{StepCoh}=\frac{\sum_{\ell}w_{\ell}\,\mathrm{sc}(\ell)}{\sum_{\ell}w_{\ell}}, 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 where w ℓ = ∑ ( i , j ) ∈ M [ gt st ​ ( c i ⋆ ) = ℓ ] w_{\ell}=\sum_{(i,j)\in M}[\mathrm{gt_{st}}(c^{\star}_{i})=\ell] counts matches originating from ground-truth step ℓ \ell . 
 
 
 
 Merge Purity. 
 
 ( Figure 5 (d))
This metric is used to evaluate whether, in a multi-hop scenario, different causally related steps are incorrectly merged. For total alignment weight between ground-truth step a a and predicted step b b : 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 W a ​ b = ∑ ( i , j ) ∈ M \displaystyle W_{ab}=\sum_{(i,j)\in M} 
 [ gt st ​ ( c i ⋆ ) = a ] ​ [ pred st ​ ( c ^ j ) = b ] ​ S i ​ j , \displaystyle[\mathrm{gt_{st}}(c^{\star}_{i})=a]\ [\mathrm{pred_{st}}(\hat{c}_{j})=b]\ S_{ij}, 
 
 (4) 
 
 
 
 
 
 where [ ⋅ ] [\cdot] denotes the Iverson bracket (indicator function), equal to (1) when its condition is true and (0) otherwise. Define column sums S b = ∑ a W a ​ b S_{b}=\sum_{a}W_{ab} , total mass S = ∑ b S b S=\sum_{b}S_{b} , and column prior P ​ ( b ) = S b / S P(b)=S_{b}/S . The conditional distribution of ground-truth steps given a predicted step is q a ∣ b = W a ​ b / S b q_{a\mid b}=W_{ab}/S_{b} for active columns with S b > 0 S_{b}>0 . The normalized purity is: 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 MergePur = 1 − H ​ ( G ∣ P ) log ⁡ G act \displaystyle\mathrm{MergePur}=1-\frac{H(G\mid P)}{\log G_{\mathrm{act}}} 
 
 (5) 
 
 
 
 
 H ​ ( G ∣ P ) = ∑ b : S b > 0 P ​ ( b ) ​ ( − ∑ a : W a ​ b > 0 q a ∣ b ​ log ⁡ q a ∣ b ) , \displaystyle H(G\mid P)=\sum_{b:S_{b}>0}P(b)\Big(-\sum_{a:W_{ab}>0}q_{a\mid b}\log q_{a\mid b}\Big), 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 where G act = | { a : ∑ b W a ​ b > 0 } | G_{\mathrm{act}}=|\{a:\sum_{b}W_{ab}>0\}| counts active ground-truth steps, H ​ ( ⋅ ) H(\cdot) uses natural logarithm, and larger values indicate predicted steps concentrating mass on single ground-truth steps (i.e., less over-merging);
 G G denotes the random variable of ground-truth step indices and P P denotes the random variable of predicted step indices; G act = | { a : ∑ b W a ​ b > 0 } | G_{\mathrm{act}}=|\{a:\sum_{b}W_{ab}>0\}| is the number of active ground-truth steps. The normalization by log ⁡ G act \log G_{\mathrm{act}} maps [ 0 , log ⁡ G act ] [0,\log G_{\mathrm{act}}] to [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1] . 
 
 
 
 Order Consistency. 
 
 ( Figure 5 (e))
This metric is used to evaluate whether, in a multi-hop scenario, the order of causally related calls is incorrectly swapped, capturing order consistency across steps. Let each match ( i , j ) ∈ M (i,j)\in M be represented by its step pair ( a , b ) = ( gt st ​ ( c i ⋆ ) , pred st ​ ( c ^ j ) ) (a,b)=(\mathrm{gt_{st}}(c^{\star}_{i}),\mathrm{pred_{st}}(\hat{c}_{j})) . Over all unordered pairs of matches that are comparable in both axes, we compute: 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 OrdCons = 1 − 1 Q ​ ∑ ( u , v ) [ ( a u − a v ) ​ ( b u − b v ) < 0 ] , \displaystyle\mathrm{OrdCons}=1-\frac{1}{Q}\sum_{(u,v)}\big[(a_{u}-a_{v})(b_{u}-b_{v})<0\big], 
 
 (6) 
 
 
 
 
 
 where the sum runs over pairs with a u ≠ a v a_{u}\neq a_{v} and b u ≠ b v b_{u}\neq b_{v} , Q Q is the number of such pairs, and the indicator records inversions between the ground-truth and predicted step orders; higher values indicate fewer inversions. 
 
 
 To penalize degenerate cases with very few matches (weak systems that match only a few easy MCP tools from inflating structure-aware metrics, leading to a very small denominator in the metric calculation, and thus an inflated outcome), we weight per-sample metrics by their recall. Let the per-sample recall be
 r m = | M m | N gt , m r_{m}\,=\,\frac{|M_{m}|}{N_{\mathrm{gt},m}}\, . For any per-sample metric F m ∈ { StepCoh , MergePur , OrdCons } F_{m}\in\{\mathrm{StepCoh},\allowbreak\ \mathrm{MergePur},\allowbreak\ \mathrm{OrdCons}\} , we report a recall-covered score:
 F cov = ∑ m N gt , m ​ r m ​ F m ∑ m N gt , m F^{\mathrm{cov}}\,=\,\frac{\sum_{m}N_{\mathrm{gt},m}\,r_{m}\,F_{m}}{\sum_{m}N_{\mathrm{gt},m}}\, . 
 
 
 
 
 
 4.4 LLMs Judges Metrics 
 
 To leverage the advantages of large language models (LLMs) in complex logical reasoning, we introduce a metric based on LLMs Judges to assess:
 
 
 
 1. 
 
 Whether the MLLM achieves Task Completion ; 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 Whether the answer provided by the MLLM possesses MCP Information Grounding . 
 
 
 
 
 
 Task Completion. 
 
 For each instance m m , a fixed set of four LLMs Judges 𝒥 \mathcal{J} produces scalar scores { s m ( j ) } j ∈ 𝒥 ⊂ [ 0 , 1 ] \{s^{(j)}_{m}\}_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\subset[0,1] . We compute a trimmed mean that discards the highest and lowest scores and averages the remaining two (Prompt: Appendix subsection 6.2 ): 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 TaskComp m = 1 2 ​ ∑ j ∈ 𝒥 ∖ { j min , j max } s m ( j ) , \displaystyle\mathrm{TaskComp}_{m}\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\!\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}\setminus\{j_{\min},\,j_{\max}\}}s^{(j)}_{m}\,, 
 
 (7) 
 
 
 
 
 j min = arg ⁡ min j ∈ 𝒥 ⁡ s m ( j ) , j max = arg ⁡ max j ∈ 𝒥 ⁡ s m ( j ) . \displaystyle j_{\min}=\arg\min_{j\in\mathcal{J}}s^{(j)}_{m},\ j_{\max}=\arg\max_{j\in\mathcal{J}}s^{(j)}_{m}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 We report TaskComp \mathrm{TaskComp} (Task Completion) as the mean of per-instance scores over the evaluation set. The judge pool includes strong large language models ( e.g . , GPT, Gemini, DeepSeek, Grok). 
 
 
 
 Information Grounding. 
 
 Judges are additionally shown both the ground-truth and predicted trajectories and emit step-level groundedness scores. For instance m m with ground-truth steps indexed by ℓ ⋆ \ell^{\star} and ℓ ^ \hat{\ell} , each judge j ∈ 𝒥 j\in\mathcal{J} outputs g m , ℓ ⋆ , ℓ ^ ( j ) ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] g^{(j)}_{m,\ell^{\star},\hat{\ell}}\in[0,1] . The calculation of InfoGrnd m , ℓ ⋆ , ℓ ^ \mathrm{InfoGrnd}_{m,\ell^{\star},\hat{\ell}} follows LABEL:eq:taskcompletion (Prompt: Appendix subsection 6.2 ).

 Table 4 : Average interaction steps and tool calls per task across models on our benchmark. 
 
 
 As shown in Table 2 , we evaluate SOTA MLLMs on M 3 -Bench. The three flagship families, GPT-5 [ 32 ] , Gemini , and Grok , form the leading cluster under our coverage-weighted alignment and judge signals. GPT-5 [ 32 ] attains the highest Average Score (0.482) with strong Recall (0.627) and high Argument Similarity (0.583), and it also tops all structure-aware metrics: its multi-threaded Step Coherence (0.502) and multi-hop sensitive Order Consistency / Merge Purity (0.290 / 0.453) indicate that it preserves both within-step parallel branches and cross-step merges in long tool graphs. Gemini 2.5 Pro [ 8 ] (0.423) and Grok 4 (0709) [ 47 ] (0.411) exhibit similarly balanced profiles. 
 
 
 In contrast, compact/latency-oriented variants tend to trade coverage and structural consistency for speed: for example, Claude 4.5 Haiku [ 3 ] achieves high Precision (0.480) but much lower Recall, and its structure metrics remain modest, pointing to short, locally correct trajectories that under-utilize multi-threaded branches and often break multi-hop dependencies. Finally, GLM 4.5v [ 15 ] , Qwen2.5-VL-72B [ 6 ] , and InternVL 3.5 [ 44 ] lag notably on both coverage and structure, suggesting that MLLMs SFT objectives favor pure vision–language understanding over instruction following and trajectory planning. 
 
 
 
 
 5.2 Per-Task Analysis 
 
 Figure 7 : Average score of each selected task (details in subsection 6.1 ) in the representative model. 
 
 
 Figure 7 shows that model capabilities are far from uniform across selected tasks (details in subsection 6.1 ): curves cross frequently, indicating complementary strengths rather than a single, uniformly dominant system. Grok-4 is weak on Fruit Cal (numerical calibration/unit handling) yet strong on QR-code / Attraction (optical character recognition (OCR) and points-of-interest (POI) chaining). GPT-5 trails on Vision Research (fine-grained figure grounding) but dominates Biology Cut (precise localization/cropping). Gemini 2.5 Pro excels on Travel Comparison (web aggregation and itinerary synthesis). Claude 4.5 lags on vision-dependent tasks (e.g., Items Price ), suggesting comparatively weaker multimodal grounding; disparities align with differences in OCR robustness. In section 9 (See Appendix), we present the performance of different models on each task, based on the evaluation metrics of M 3 -Bench. 
 
 
 
 
 5.3 Budgets across models. 
 
 Table 3 complements these interaction statistics by reporting average per-task input and output token budgets. Table 4 summarizes the average interaction depth per task. Larger models often pursue longer, multi-hop plans: GPT-5 [ 32 ] and GPT-5 Nano [ 31 ] average (3.55 / 14.77) and (5.13 / 17.97) (steps / tool calls), indicating frequent retries and non-trivial orchestration; Llama-4-Scout-17B16E [ 26 ] and Qwen2.5-VL-72B [ 6 ] show similarly heavy tool use. By contrast, Gemini 2.5 Pro [ 8 ] (3.04 / 6.95), Gemini 2.5 Flash [ 8 ] (4.04 / 7.12), and Grok-4 Fast [ 46 ] (2.91 / 5.93) reach goals with fewer, more precise calls, consistent with their higher detection precision. Very short dialogues ( e.g . , Claude 4.5 Haiku [ 3 ] , GLM 4.5v [ 15 ] ) reflect under-coverage rather than efficiency. 
 
 
 
 
 5.4 Analysis of Error Distributions 
 
 We assess per-call reliability by replaying each model’s trajectory through the MCP interpreter and categorizing every tool_call into one of five mutually exclusive outcomes shown in Figure 6 : (i) Illegal calling format : the MCP request cannot be parsed into a valid schema ( e.g . , missing name or arguments ); (ii) Unknown Tool Invocation : the request targets a non-existent or non-exposed tool; (iii) Invalid Invocation Arguments : the tool is found but arguments are syntactically/semantically invalid (typically surface as HTTP 400 or “[Tool error] Invalid arguments”); (iv) Successful (Resource Not Found) : the call is structurally correct yet the backend cannot route/locate the requested resource ( e.g . , HTTP 404); and (v) Successful call : the tool executes and returns a normal result. These categories allow us to decouple schema conformance, tool selection, argument fidelity, and downstream retrieval robustness. 
 
 
 Overall, higher-performing systems allocate a larger share to Successful call in Figure 6 , with the GPT-5 Grok and Gemini families exhibiting the strongest success rates in our setting. For Qwen2.5-VL-72B [ 6 ] and GLM-4.5V [ 15 ] , the shortfall appears driven less by multi-hop reasoning capacity and more by tool-use hygiene: hallucination-triggered invocations of non-existent tools and weaker adherence to instruction/schema constraints (the trajectory error analysis in Appendix Error: GLM-4.5v , Error: Qwen2.5-VL , Error: Internvl-3.5 ). This pattern suggests that improvements in schema guidance and instruction following would likely yield larger gains than refining high-level reasoning alone.
Although the Claude family is generally regarded as possessing strong capabilities in coding, mathematics, and reasoning, its cross-modal prior knowledge is not particularly prominent section 8 . 
 
 
 
 
 
 6 Conclusion 
 
 We introduced M 3 -Bench, a benchmark tailored to multimodal tool use under the Model Context Protocol (MCP), with trajectories that support multi-hop and multi-threaded execution. Our evaluation pipeline aligns predicted and reference calls by serializing each call, embedding it with a text encoder, and performing tool-name–bucketed Hungarian matching to obtain auditable one-to-one correspondences. On this alignment, we report a structure-aware suite of interpretable metrics.
Across representative MLLMs, results reveal persistent gaps in argument fidelity and structural consistency, highlighting the need for agents that jointly reason over images, text, and tool graphs.

 Supplementary Material 
 
 
 
 
 6.1 Task Descriptions 
 
 (0) Items Price 
 
 Estimate prices for all visible retail items. 
 
 
 1. 
 
 Identify product names from the image (OCR/visual grounding as needed). 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 Use Amazon MCP (search_products) to retrieve current prices. 
 
 
 
 3. 
 
 Aggregate results into a concise table. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 (1) Attraction 
 
 Resolve the attraction, surface context, and plan around-visit options. 
 
 
 1. 
 
 Use Wikipedia MCP to obtain the city/background of the attraction. 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 Call weather/get_weather for current conditions. 
 
 
 
 3. 
 
 Use google-maps/places_nearby_search to find nearby restaurants. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 (2) Movies 
 
 Identify the movie and retrieve showtimes contextually. 
 
 
 1. 
 
 Use OCR to read the movie title in the image. 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 Query TMDB to disambiguate the title. 
 
 
 
 3. 
 
 Search cinemas near New York showing the resolved film via Google Maps MCP. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 (3) Vision Research 
 
 Bridge detected fauna to literature snippets. 
 
 
 1. 
 
 Detect animals and identify two distinct categories from detections. 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 For each, call paper_search/search_arxiv with a targeted query. 
 
 
 
 3. 
 
 Download and read the paper; extract the shortest Abstract sentence containing the animal name. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 (4) Camping 
 
 Plan a 3-day national-park camping itinerary with verified amenities and logistics. 
 
 
 1. 
 
 Use nationalparks/getCampgrounds and nationalparks/getAlerts to select an open campground with ≥ \geq 3 amenities and no active alerts. 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 Call nationalparks/getVisitorCenters and pick the closest visitor center; record hours. 
 
 
 
 3. 
 
 Use google-maps/compute_route, distance_matrix, and elevation to compute routes and elevation; compile the itinerary. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 (5) Fish 
 
 Plan a New Jersey fishing trip for the detected species. 
 
 
 1. 
 
 Use wiki/summary to summarize the detected fish species. 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 Use wiki/search to search for the top spot for fishing in NJ recently. 
 
 
 
 3. 
 
 Call weather/get_weather for local conditions at the spot. 
 
 
 
 4. 
 
 Use google-maps/compute_route to plan a route from a default start. 
 
 
 
 5. 
 
 Use google-maps/places_nearby_search to search for restaurants and bait shops. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 (6) Drug 
 
 Compare medicines for a given condition safely and conveniently. 
 
 
 1. 
 
 Look up drug facts via fda_drug_lookup MCP. 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 Search similar products via Amazon (search_products). 
 
 
 
 3. 
 
 Create a comparison list using Excel MCP. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 (7) Hazard 
 
 Document construction-safety hazards with visual annotations and slides. 
 
 
 1. 
 
 Run a detector to find hardhats, vests, ladders, and unguarded edges. 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 Use imagesorcery-mcp/draw_circles to annotate detections. 
 
 
 
 3. 
 
 Create a presentation with ppt/create_presentation, ppt/add_slide and ppt/manage_image. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 (8) Food Reddit Wiki Excel 
 
 Quantify food popularity and descriptions, then export to Excel. 
 
 
 1. 
 
 For each food, query Reddit MCP for the hottest post and extract comment count. 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 Use Wiki MCP to get a brief description. 
 
 
 
 3. 
 
 Create a workbook and write two columns (Food, Wikipedia Description) via Excel MCP; save the file. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 (9) MedExcel 
 
 Summarize PubMed signal into key terms in Excel. 
 
 
 1. 
 
 Use pubmed_search to retrieve the first three titles for the drug/disease. 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 Double-check whether each title is semantically relevant to the queried drug/disease. 
 
 
 
 3. 
 
 Write the drug/disease name and the relevant paper count to an Excel file under ./media and save. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 (10) Barcode Lookup 
 
 Identify the book via barcode and fetch bibliographic data. 
 
 
 1. 
 
 Decode the barcode using pyzbar. 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 Query openlibrary_mcp to return the book record. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 (11) Biology Cut 
 
 Produce one image per detected animal instance. 
 
 
 1. 
 
 Detect all animals in the image. 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 Crop each detection to an individual image. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 (12) Fruit Cal 
 
 Assess daily calories and potential long-term risks from fruit-only meals. 
 
 
 1. 
 
 Use Nutrition MCP to estimate total daily calories from the shown fruits. 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 Use Healthcare MCP to assess long-term health concerns of such a diet. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 (13) Medical 
 
 Recommend safe medicines with purchase links conditioned on ICD-10. 
 
 
 1. 
 
 Use lookupICDCode MCP to validate the ICD-10 code. 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 Use fda_drug_lookup and clinical_trials_search to check safety/compatibility. 
 
 
 
 3. 
 
 Suggest appropriate options via Amazon MCP with links. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 (14) QR Attraction 
 
 Infer location from a QR-sourced page and report air quality and guidance. 
 
 
 1. 
 
 Use a QR scanner tool to extract the URL; fetch the primary image. 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 Infer the location and geocode it via Google Maps MCP. 
 
 
 
 3. 
 
 Query google-air/current_conditions for AQI and health recommendations. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 (15) Routine 
 
 Plan a multi-stop route with a supermarket stop and choose the fastest option. 
 
 
 1. 
 
 Geocode the start, the image landmark, and the destination via Google Maps MCP. 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 Search for nearby supermarkets as waypoints. 
 
 
 
 3. 
 
 Compare ≥ \geq 3 candidate routes (compute_route or directions) and select the fastest. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 (16) Biology Chain 
 
 Evaluate whether detected organisms can form a plausible food chain. 
 
 
 1. 
 
 Detect all organisms; blur any human instances. 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 Use Wikipedia to retrieve ecological roles (diet, trophic level). 
 
 
 
 3. 
 
 Check whether roles form a valid chain. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 (17) Generate PowerPoint 
 
 Recreate a title slide with style and optional Q&A enhancement. 
 
 
 1. 
 
 OCR the slide and segment into blocks; estimate font color/size/style. 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 Call create_presentation to get presentation_id; use create_slide_from_template to build the slide. 
 
 
 
 3. 
 
 If a question is present, fetch and summarize Reddit opinions for a Q&A slide. 
 
 
 
 4. 
 
 save_presentation to output PPTX. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 (18) Travel Comparison 
 
 Choose the cleaner destination between two landmarks and suggest nearby venues. 
 
 
 1. 
 
 Recognize both landmarks and geocode each via Google Maps MCP. 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 Use google-air/current_conditions to compare AQI and pollutant levels. 
 
 
 
 3. 
 
 Recommend the cleaner destination; list nearby cinemas and hotels. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 (19) Heatmap 
 
 Generate an air-pollution heatmap visualization for the depicted city. 
 
 
 1. 
 
 Geocode the city via Google Maps MCP. 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 Render a static base map (static_map) with zoom=10. 
 
 
 
 3. 
 
 Overlay PM2.5 heatmap tiles via air-quality/heatmap_tile (indexType=US_AQI). 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 (20) Museum 
 
 Retrieve a Met object and attach a concise Wikipedia summary. 
 
 
 1. 
 
 Call metmuseum-mcp/list-departments and select the best-aligned departmentId. 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 Call metmuseum-mcp/search-museum-objects (hasImages=true, departmentId) and select an objectId. 
 
 
 
 3. 
 
 Call metmuseum-mcp/get-museum-object to get title, classification, and image URL. 
 
 
 
 4. 
 
 Use wiki/search then wiki/summary to obtain a short cultural summary. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 (21) Research Station 
 
 Relate solar flare events to nearby research stations for the inferred location. 
 
 
 1. 
 
 Use nasa-mcp/get_notifications to filter M5+ flares within the specified window. 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 For each event date, call get_solar_flare and get_coronal_mass_ejection; store flux/peak_time and CME IDs/types. 
 
 
 
 3. 
 
 Infer photo location; use google-maps-mcp/places_text_search and place_details to list top research stations and compile a table. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 (22) Hugeicons 
 
 Map salient objects to Wikipedia titles and validated icons. 
 
 
 1. 
 
 Use dinox-mcp/detect-all-objects and keep detections with area > \textgreater 10,000. 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 For each object, call wiki/search_wikipedia to get an article title. 
 
 
 
 3. 
 
 Call hugeicons/search_icons and hugeicons/list_icons; cross-validate up to two icon names per object. 
 
 
 
 4. 
 
 Output a JSON table with Object, Wikipedia Title, and Icon Names. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 (23) Crypto Purchase Estimation 
 
 Estimate how many main products 100 units of the most volatile currency can buy. 
 
 
 1. 
 
 Use dinox-mcp/detect-all-objects and select the object with the largest bounding box as the main product. 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 Query amazon/search_products for price/title; compute 24h volatilities from okx-mcp/get_candlesticks across candidate instruments and pick the most volatile. 
 
 
 
 3. 
 
 Get current price and 24h volume via okx-mcp/get_price. 
 
 
 
 4. 
 
 Compute purchasing power and estimated items purchasable; return structured JSON. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 (24) Stock Growth Estimation 
 
 Estimate the short-term growth potential of the companies and annotate the image with the final result. 
 
 
 1. 
 
 Use yahoo-finance/get_stock_info to retrieve real-time prices for a group of candidate company tickers and keep the two highest-priced companies for further analysis. 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 For selected companies, call yahoo-finance/get_historical_stock_prices over 2024-10-01 to 2024-10-03, compute the average of their daily Close prices, and keep the company with higher average for further analysis. 
 
 
 
 3. 
 
 Call yahoo-finance/get_recommendations for the final selected company and compute the upgrade ratio. Then compute the short-term growth percentage using the formula:
Growth Percentage = Real-time Price − Average Close Price Average Close Price × 100 \frac{\text{Real-time Price}-\text{Average Close Price}}{\text{Average Close Price}}\times 100 + + (Upgrade Ratio × 10 \times 10 ). 
 
 
 
 4. 
 
 Use imagesorcery-mcp/draw_texts to write the computed growth estimate onto the input image, and return the resulting ticker, its price metrics, the upgrade ratio, the estimated growth, and the annotated image. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 (25) FIPE Car Variant Lookup 
 
 Identify the vehicle, determine its 20xx FIPE variant, and summarize its fuel type. 
 
 
 1. 
 
 Identify the vehicle and extract both the brand name and model keywords. Query car-price/get_car_brands for the complete list of supported brands. Call okx/get_exchange_rate to obtain the current BRL→USD and BRL→BTC conversion rates. 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 If the brand exists in the supported list, then use its brand name and model keywords, call car-price/search_brand_model_price to retrieve all matching FIPE entries, filter these results to keep only the 20xx variant. 
 
 
 
 3. 
 
 Use wiki/summary to look up the fuel type of the 20xx variant, and provide a short description based on the search result. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 (26) Class Demand Estimation 
 
 Estimate class demand, compare it with the detected object count, and determine whether the class budget is sufficient. 
 
 
 1. 
 
 Use Math-MCP tools math/mean and math/median to compute median and mode from the collected survey statistic {6, 8, …}. Use dinox-mcp/detect-all-objects to detect all objects and identify the most frequently appearing object category. Compare the survey median, the survey mode, and the detected top object count, then take the largest among them as the estimated demand. 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 Use amazon/search_products to query the detected top object category, obtain the first returned product, and compute the total cost as the ceiling of the estimated demand multiplied by the item price. 
 
 
 
 3. 
 
 If the total cost is within the class budget of xxx dollars budget, call Reddit-MCP-Server/search_hot_posts to fetch the first recent hot post and wiki/summary to obtain a short description for the detected object category. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 (27) NixOS Diagnostic 
 
 Perform a staged NixOS diagnostic workflow using OCR, NixOS tools, and Wiki tools. 
 
 
 1. 
 
 Use ocr/perform_ocr exactly once to extract the package names appearing in the code on the image. These package names will be used in the following steps. 
 
 
 
 2. 
 
 Take the first five package names in their original order and call nixos/nixos_search once for each of them to check whether they exist in NixOS. If fewer than three of these packages exist, stop the workflow and report. 
 
 
 
 3. 
 
 Otherwise, collect the first three package names that do exist. Call nixos/nixos_info on services.openssh.enable. Then call nixos/home_manager_search with the query “zsh” and keep the first three returned options; choose the first one and call nixos/home_manager_info. Finally, call wiki/summary once for each of the three existing packages to provide brief background information. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 6.2 LLM Prompts Used in Executor and Judge 
 
 Prepare (Tool Planning) 
 
 Following the planning protocol described in Experiment subsection 3.1 , the Executor first selects (but does not execute) the tool set for the current step. The exact prompt template used for this stage is given below. 
 
 
 Prepare (Tool Planning) 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Process (Controlled Execution) 
 
 Given the selected tools, execution proceeds under controlled, JSON-only invocation with bounded parallelism as specified in Experiment subsection 3.1 . The prompt below instantiates that protocol. 
 
 
 Process (Controlled Execution) 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Stop/Continu 
 
 A binary stopping criterion gates the transition to summarization, as defined in Experiment subsection 3.1 . The following prompt enforces a strict yes/no decision. 
 
 
 Stop/Continu 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Final Reply 
 
 When the stopping criterion is met, the agent emits only the final natural-language answer (Experiment subsection 3.1 ). The corresponding prompt template is provided below. 
 
 
 Final Reply 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Judge 
 
 The Judge independently audits causal order and factual correctness, per Experiment subsection 3.1 . The prompt below operationalizes this decision. 
 
 
 Judge 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Summarize 
 
 Upon success, the Judge reconstructs an optimal trajectory by merging/removing calls and grouping parallel operations (Experiment subsection 3.1 ). We provide the standard summarization prompt below. 
 
 
 Summarize 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Task Completion 
 
 For end-to-end task completion, we use a four-judge trimmed-mean score defined in Experiment subsection 4.4 . The prompt below elicits the required scalar. 
 
 
 Task Completion 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Information Grounding 
 
 Step-level groundedness is evaluated against the reference steps as defined in Experiment subsection 4.4 . The prompt below collects the coverage-only score. 
 
 
 Information Grounding 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 6.3 Bucketed Hungarian sample pairs based on similarity 
 
 To make the alignment intuition concrete, Figure 8 shows three representative call pairs after canonicalization and sentence-encoder cosine scoring. Within each tool bucket, only pairs clearing a weak screen τ weak \tau_{\text{weak}} are eligible for Hungarian assignment; those above a stronger bar τ strong \tau_{\text{strong}} are treated as high-confidence matches. The high/medium/low examples illustrate how semantically close arguments receive credit despite surface variation, while dissimilar pairs are withheld—clarifying how our Argument Similarity feeds the bucketed matching used in subsection 4.1 and, downstream, the structure-aware metrics. 
 
 
 
 
 
 (a) High similarity 
 
 
 
 
 
 (b) Medium similarity 
 
 
 
 
 
 (c) Low similarity 
 
 
 
 Figure 8 : Illustrative call-level similarity under our sentence-encoder + cosine metric with bucketed Hungarian matching: three representative pairs exhibiting high, medium, and low similarity.
This visualization contextualizes Argument Similarity and the weak/strong thresholding used for alignment (see subsection 4.1 ; cf. related agent evaluation protocols. 
 
 
 
 6.4 Human Study 
 
 We conducted a small-scale user study with 30 Ph.D. and researchers working with Model Context Protocol (MCP) tools to validate two design choices: (i) the use of human prompts and human judges (see subsection 4.4 ) for obtaining an optimal trajectory; and (ii) the similarity thresholding used together with the bucketed Hungarian (Kuhn–Munkres) matching (see subsection 4.1 ). 
 
 
 Preference for Optimal Trajectory. 
 
 Each participant received 20 randomly sampled pairs of trajectories (the original MLLM-produced trajectory versus the human-optimized Best Trajectory ), with order randomized and content blinded. Participants chose the better one per pair based on faithfulness and procedural soundness. Overall, the Best Trajectory was preferred in 98.16% of cases; under our pairwise setup this corresponds to 589/600 selections (98.17%). 
 
 
 
 Calibration of similarity thresholds. 
 
 From the evaluation pool, we selected 40 ground-truth/predicted call pairs and asked participants to categorize them as strong , medium , or weak correlation (majority vote). We then swept the similarity thresholds and found that ( τ strong , τ weak ) = ( 0.8 , 0.6 ) (\tau_{\mathrm{strong}},\tau_{\mathrm{weak}})=(0.8,0.6) produced the closest agreement with human labels. 
 
 
 
 7 Trajectory Case Study 
 
 Format. We present each trajectory using the two-column key–value layout defined in trajectoryformat.tex . Labels in the left column are typeset in monospace. IMG denotes the input image and Q the task query. Step k indicates the k k -th multi-hop step in the execution. Within a step, dashed separators denote parallel (multi-threaded) tool calls. Each call reports Tool (invoked tool), ARG (arguments), and OUT (returned output). The final answer is summarized under Final Reply . 
 
 
 Object detection with per-instance cropping; parallel crop operations produce one image per detected animal. See (11) Biology Cut .

 Fish

 Example: Saltwater fish ID and NJ trip planning 
 
 
   
 
 
 
 
 IMG 
 
 
 
 
 
 Q 
 
 From the NJ fish photo, can you ID the species with a brief overview, pick a recent good spot to try, check the current weather there, map a route from the usual starting point, and list nearby restaurants and a bait shop within about 5 km? 
 
 
   
 
 
 Step 1 
 
 
   
 
 
 
 Tool 
 
 wiki/summary 
 
 
 
 ARG 
 
 title="American eel" 
 
 
 
 OUT 
 
 Species identified as American eel ( Anguilla rostrata ); catadromous, slender body, continuous dorsal/anal fin, nocturnal feeder common along Atlantic coast and NJ estuaries. 
 
 
 
 
 
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   
 
 
 
 Tool 
 
 wiki/search 
 
 
 
 ARG 
 
 query="Sandy Hook fishing New Jersey"; n=5 
 
 
 
 OUT 
 
 Sandy Hook (Gateway NRA, near Highlands, NJ) highlighted as productive surf/shore area for eels and other inshore species. 
 
 
   
 
 
 Step 2 
 
 
   
 
 
 
 Tool 
 
 weather/get_weather 
 
 
 
 ARG 
 
 location="Highlands, NJ"; units="us" 
 
 
 
 OUT 
 
 Current conditions around Highlands, NJ show mild temperatures ( 52.2°F) and light winds ( 4.1 mph), suitable for an evening shore session. 
 
 
   
 
 
 Step 3 
 
 
   
 
 
 
 Tool 
 
 google-maps/compute_route 
 
 
 
 ARG 
 
 origin={latLng:{40.7357,-74.1724}}; destination={latLng:{40.4333,-73.9885}}; travelMode = DRIVE; fields = routes.distanceMeters, … 
 
 
 
 OUT 
 
 Route from Newark, NJ to Sandy Hook, NJ computed; distance ∼ \sim 44 miles, duration ∼ \sim 1 hour via Garden State Parkway and NJ-36 S. 
 
 
   
 
 
 Step 4 
 
 
   
 
 
 
 Tool 
 
 google-maps/places_nearby_search 
 
 
 
 ARG 
 
 location={40.4333,-73.9885}; radiusMeters=5000; includedTypes=["restaurant","fishing_store"]; languageCode="en"; regionCode="US" 
 
 
 
 OUT 
 
 Nearby options include several waterfront restaurants (e.g., Bahrs Landing, The Proving Ground, Off The Hook, One Willow) and bait/tackle shops within 5 km. 
 
 
   
 
 
 Final Reply 
 
 
   
 
 
 Species: American eel ( Anguilla rostrata ); catadromous, active at night in tidal creeks/marshes. Spot: Sandy Hook, NJ (Gateway NRA near Highlands). Weather: calm, cool, fishable. Route: Newark→Sandy Hook = 44 mi /  1 h. Amenities: multiple waterfront restaurants and local bait & tackle within  5 km. Plan: fish evening high/outgoing tides along bayside creek mouths and rock structures; use bloodworms or cut bait; register with NJ Saltwater Recreational Registry and follow current regulations. 
 
 
 
   
 
 
 
 Generate Powerpoint

 QR decoded to Unsplash link. 
 Main image downloaded successfully (Nick Fewings). 
 Detected location: Natural History Museum, London (51.496715, -0.1763672). 
 Current Air Quality Index (UAQI): 82 — Excellent. Dominant pollutant: NO 2 . 
 Health guidance: All individuals may continue outdoor activity safely; those with asthma or NO 2 sensitivity should still avoid prolonged exposure to traffic-heavy roads. Ventilation and light exercise remain safe.

 Organisms noted
 -- Grass/green plants — producers (photosynthesis).
 -- Trees — producers.
 -- Rabbit — herbivore; primary consumer.
 -- Fox — omnivore/mesopredator; secondary consumer.
 -- Wolf — apex carnivore; tertiary consumer.
 -- Person — human omnivore (not part of wild chain). 
 
 
 Valid food chain?
 Yes. Examples:
 1) Grass → \rightarrow Rabbit → \rightarrow Wolf (common).
 2) Grass → \rightarrow Rabbit → \rightarrow Fox → \rightarrow Wolf (ecologically possible; wolves often kill foxes as competitors and may occasionally consume them). 
 
 
 Plants act as producers; rabbit as primary consumer; fox as secondary; wolf as tertiary/apex. The organisms present can form a valid trophic sequence. 
 
 
 
   
 
 
 
 8 Error Trajectory Distribution Analysis 
 
 We illustrate representative failure trajectories to analyze the characteristic error patterns of different multimodal large language models (MLLMs). Each case is presented following the same format. 
 
 
 GLM-4.5v 
 
 In the Fruit Cal task, glm-4.5v exhibits an Illegal calling format : the reply is wrapped with non-JSON delimiters, violating the MCP schema for name / arguments . This malformed structure prevents any valid tool execution and halts progress.
 
 
 
   
 
 
 Illegal calling format 
 Task: Fruit Cal 
 Model: glm-4.5v 
 
 
   
 
 
 
 
 IMG 
 
 
 
 
 
 Q 
 
 If a woman ate only the fruits shown here for all three meals, would that cover minimum daily calories, and what brief long-term health concerns might that raise? 
   
 
 
 Final Reply 
 
 
   
 
 
 "<|begin_of_box|>""name":"food_mcp/get_nutrition"
"arguments":{"query":"1 watermelon"}"<|end_of_box|> ..." 
 
 
 Unconventional invocation format using "<|begin_of_box|> ... <|end_of_box|>" 
 
 
 
   
 
 
 
 Qwen2.5-VL-72B-Instruct 
 
 In the Movies task, Qwen2.5-VL-72B-Instruct repeatedly issues Unknown Tool Invocation to ocr/perform and tmdb/* endpoints absent from the registry, and later misuses a generic server/tool wrapper. Without tool discovery or correction, the trajectory culminates in an unsupported final claim.
